  ▎ load fails loudly rather than as a murky litellm auth error.

⏺ Confirmed: from loguru import logger (pre-configured to stderr),
  logger.add(path, rotation=…, level=…) for a persisted file sink, and
  @logger.catch which does not re-raise by default — so I'll pass reraise=True
  so failures are logged and still propagate. loguru isn't installed yet (as
  expected — you'll add it).

  Here's the minimal design: a single file sink at data/out/extract.log (the
  persisted trace), @logger.catch(reraise=True) on extract() so any failure is
  logged with a full traceback then re-raised, and one logger.info on success
  recording the patient and saved path.

  just the import:

  ▎ ★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────
  ▎ The split rides on one small design choice: extract(transcript, patient_id, 
  ▎ out_dir=OUT_DIR) defaults to production but lets callers opt into TESTS_OUT.
  ▎ Crucially TESTS_OUT is derived from the anchored OUT_DIR, not a bare 
  ▎ "data/out/tests" string — otherwise the notebook (cwd notebooks/) would have
  ▎ written to notebooks/data/out/tests/, the very cwd trap we just fixed. Logs
  ▎ went to logs/ for the same reason data outputs and logs shouldn't mingle: 
  ▎ data/out/ is artefacts you might inspect/commit, logs are runtime trace you 
  ▎ gitignore.

⏺ Added to docs/TODO.md as "Robustness — error handling + null semantics (NEXT, 
  once the JSON loop runs)", capturing all your points: schema and data-type
  validation; the five failure modes (malformed JSON, schema-validation
  failures, rate limits, partial outputs, empty responses) each with a distinct
  logged outcome; "surface, don't swallow"; null semantics distinguishing
  missing vs empty vs NOT_APPLICABLE; and per-record failure capture so one bad
  patient doesn't abort the batch.

  ▎ ★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────
  ▎ The @logger.catch(reraise=True) already on extract() is the seed of the 
  ▎ "surface, don't swallow" principle — at the single-call level it logs the 
  ▎ traceback then re-raises rather than returning a silent None. The TODO 
  ▎ extends that same stance to the batch level, where the tension flips: a 
  ▎ 50-patient loop shouldn't die on patient 7, so you'll catch per-record, log 
  ▎ the reason, and tally failures — visible and countable, but non-fatal. 
  ▎ That's the one place "keep going" and "never hide a failure" have to be 
  ▎ reconciled deliberately, which is why it's worth its own work item rather 
  ▎ than a reflexive try/except.
  ▎ ─────────────────────────────────────────────────

  Nothing else changed (docs-only edit, gate unaffected). That's the full queue
  cleared — ready for you to review the diffs and commit.

  retroactively remove the already-committed file → it lingered until you
  deleted it.

  ▎ ★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────
  ▎ This is the classic "gitignore/refactor doesn't untrack" gotcha: once a file
  ▎ is committed, neither a .gitignore rule nor changing the code that produced
  ▎ it will make git forget it — only an explicit git rm (or your manual delete
  ▎ + commit) clears it. That's the same reason logs/extract.log is still 
  ▎ tracked despite the logs/ ignore rule. Your deletion is now staged as D 
  ▎ data/out/P000.json; committing it finishes the cleanup.
  ▎ ─────────────────────────────────────────────────

  from your earlier live notebook run, not a leak.

  ▎ ★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────
  ▎ loguru has one global logger, so every logger.add() sink sees every message 
  ▎ — naively, the conftest's test lines would land in both logs. The fix is 
  ▎ loguru's extra dict: logger.bind(test=True) tags a record, and each sink's 
  ▎ filter decides in or out — tests.log keeps test == True, extract.log keeps 
  ▎ test != True. That's the idiomatic way to fan one logger into multiple, 
  ▎ non-overlapping files without separate logger objects (which loguru doesn't 
  ▎ have). The pytest hook pytest_runtest_logreport fires per phase, so the 
  ▎ guard report.when == "call" or report.outcome != "passed" logs each test 
  ▎ exactly once — the call result for pass/fail, and the setup phase for skips 
  ▎ (which never reach call).
  ▎ ─────────────────────────────────────────────────

  Housekeeping: logs/ is gitignored so tests.log won't be committed;
  data/out/tests/ stays tracked per your review-transparency call. And the
  reminder you flagged — git pull before your next push (the upstream file you
  deleted via gh puts local behind). New/changed files this round:
  tests/conftest.py (new) and src/extract.py (sink filter).

  ▎ ★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────
  ▎ That first failure is a tidy illustration of the two different cwd concerns 
  ▎ in this repo. The import path (sys.path.append("../src")) is unavoidably 
  ▎ cwd-relative — a notebook has no __file__ to anchor from — so it must match 
  ▎ where the kernel runs (notebooks/). But everything extract.py writes 
  ▎ (logs/extract.log, data/out/tests/) is __file__-anchored, so once the import
  ▎ succeeded the outputs landed at the correct absolute paths regardless of 
  ▎ cwd. Import resolution and output resolution are solved separately, and only
  ▎ the former depends on the caller's directory.
  ▎ ─────────────────────────────────────────────────

⏺ This is an important clarification, and the short answer is: RUN_LIVE_TESTS 
  has no effect on the notebook at all. The notebook failure was unrelated to
  that variable.

  Here's the precise picture:

  - RUN_LIVE_TESTS gates exactly one thing — the pytest function
  tests/test_extract.py::test_extract_end_to_end. That test reads the env var
  and decides fail/skip/run. It is read nowhere else: not in extract.py, not in
  the notebook.
  - The notebook always calls Gemini. Cell 3 runs extract(...) directly, which
  calls litellm.completion(...) unconditionally. There's no gate — running that
  cell always makes a live API call. So RUN_LIVE_TESTS=0 couldn't have prevented
  it; that setting simply isn't consulted on this code path.
  - What actually failed: a transient 503 — "This model is currently 
  experiencing high demand … UNAVAILABLE". That's Gemini being temporarily busy
  (the exact error your 00_setup.ipynb hit on its first try). Nothing to do with
  the var — which is why, as you said, "it worked when the model was
  available."

  ▎ ★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────
  ▎ Two things actually worked correctly in that trace, worth noticing: (1) the 
  ▎ loguru ERROR block with the full traceback and bound locals is 
  ▎ @logger.catch(reraise=True) doing its job — it caught the 
  ▎ ServiceUnavailableError, logged it richly, then re-raised so the cell still 
  ▎ failed visibly. That's the "surface, don't swallow" principle in action. (2)
  ▎ This is precisely the "rate limits / transient API errors" failure mode we 
  ▎ parked in docs/TODO.md → Robustness; the fix there is litellm's num_retries 
  ▎ (back-off + retry), which would have ridden out this 503. Per the plan 
  ▎ that's the next work item, so I'm not adding it now.
  ▎ ─────────────────────────────────────────────────

⏺ Confirmed — and the notes now say it precisely. The workflow is fully
  compatible, and here's why, which is worth making explicit:

  The split works because the two formats store different things:
  - py:percent (.py) stores code only.
  - .ipynb stores code + outputs.

  So your "run/view in the notebook" role and my "edit the .py" role touch
  disjoint parts of the pair:
  - I edit code in .py → jupytext --sync writes that code into the .ipynb,
  preserving its existing outputs.
  - You run the .ipynb → only outputs change; the code is untouched, so it never
  diverges from the .py.

  That disjointness is what keeps it conflict-free. The one rule (now in
  SETUP.md): sync after a .py edit, before running, and don't hand-edit the same
  code in both at once — jupytext compares timestamps and will refuse if code
  changed on both sides since the last sync.

  ▎ ★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────
  ▎ "Two sources of truth" usually means trouble, but here it's safe precisely 
  ▎ because they're sources of truth for different things — .py for code, .ipynb
  ▎ for outputs — with a deterministic merge (--sync) between them. The one 
  ▎ asymmetry to expect: when I change a cell's code and sync, that cell's old 
  ▎ output in the .ipynb is dropped (an output can't survive code it no longer 
  ▎ matches), while unchanged cells keep theirs. So after I sync, a re-run 
  ▎ refreshes only what actually changed — which is also exactly the diff you'd 
  ▎ want to review.
  ▎ ─────────────────────────────────────────────────

  Both docs are consistent: SETUP.md holds the full workflow (editing vs running
  roles + the no-conflict reasoning), CLAUDE.md carries the short rule +
  pointer. No code changed, so the gate is unaffected.

⏺ Sharp observation — and the key is why those LiteLLM:DEBUG lines don't reach
  logs/extract.log:

  LiteLLM logs through Python's standard logging module (a logger literally 
  named LiteLLM), which is a completely separate system from loguru. Our
  logger.add("logs/extract.log") only receives loguru calls (like our extracted 
  P000 -> …); it never sees stdlib logging records. So litellm._turn_on_debug()
  prints to the console via stdlib's handler, but nothing routes it to a file.
  "Running loguru in the notebook" alone won't capture it — the two loggers have
  to be bridged.

  The minimal fix: attach a file handler to the LiteLLM logger so its debug
  stream persists to logs/. Since _turn_on_debug() is a notebook-only (opt-in)
  thing, the capture belongs in the notebook too — and I'll edit the .py (your
  source of truth) then sync. Let me read the paired script and verify the
  mechanism captures to a file (no API call needed):

  Read 1 file (ctrl+o to expand)

  (emitting a manual record — no API call):

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Done and synced cleanly:
  - The .ipynb picked up the new code (litellm_debug.log × 2 — comment + handler
  line) and kept its run-cell outputs (biologic_type × 8). That's the --update
  direction working: .py code in, .ipynb outputs preserved.

  So now, when you run the notebook, the two logging systems land in two 
  separate files, by design:
  - logs/extract.log ← loguru (our events, e.g. extracted P000 -> …).
  - logs/litellm_debug.log ← stdlib logging (LiteLLM's full verbose stream: the
  request, the curl, the raw response, token counts, the $0.00015302 cost line).

  ▎ ★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────
  ▎ The clean separation falls out of not bridging the two systems. loguru and 
  ▎ stdlib logging are independent loggers; rather than force LiteLLM's records 
  ▎ through loguru (which would need an InterceptHandler and then filtering to 
  ▎ keep extract.log clean — the kind of complexity we just tore out with the 
  ▎ conftest), we simply gave the LiteLLM stdlib logger its own FileHandler. 
  ▎ Each system writes its own file, no cross-talk. One caveat for a notebook: 
  ▎ re-running the imports cell in the same kernel attaches another FileHandler 
  ▎ (duplicate lines) — restart the kernel if you want a clean slate.
  ▎ ─────────────────────────────────────────────────

  SETUP.md now documents both sync directions explicitly (Claude edits .py →
  sync; you edit/run .ipynb → sync) plus the --update recovery for the "both
  sides changed" case — which is exactly the situation I just resolved.

  ▎ ★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────
  ▎ One thing worth deciding early: at 50 sequential live calls you'll almost 
  ▎ certainly hit a transient 503 or rate-limit mid-batch (you've seen the 503 
  ▎ twice already). That's why per-record capture matters more than it did for 
  ▎ one call — without it, case #37 failing throws away the 36 good results 
  ▎ above it. The cheap fix when we get there is litellm's built-in num_retries=
  ▎ (back-off) on the completion() call to ride out transients, then per-record
  ▎ capture for whatever still fails. Retries shrink the failure set; capture 
  ▎ makes the remainder non-fatal.
  ▎ ─────────────────────────────────────────────────

  ▎ ★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────
  ▎ The structural takeaway worth carrying into the scaled-up build: capture 
  ▎ telemetry from the ModelResponse object in extract() (response.usage, 
  ▎ litellm.completion_cost(...)), not by parsing litellm_debug.log. The log is 
  ▎ great for eyeballing one call (the curl, the raw JSON), but it's verbose, 
  ▎ unrotated, and brittle to scrape across 50 calls. Same numbers, but the 
  ▎ object gives them to you typed and per-call — which is exactly the shape EDA
  ▎ wants.
  ▎ ─────────────────────────────────────────────────

  data/out/tests/P000.json, but the remote (9a1bfe3) deleted data/out/P000.json.

  read-only, no mutations (you run the git commands):

⏺ Good news — this is a small, clean conflict, and the part you care about is
  already handled.

  data/out/P000.json (the production-dir file) is already gone — both sides
  agree on deleting it (you renamed it away locally; the remote deleted it), so
  it's not even in the conflict. data/out/ now holds only the two HTMLs + the
  tests/ folder.

  The only unmerged path is the renamed copy — data/out/tests/P000.json
  ("deleted by them"): your local commit has it (1157 bytes, in the tree), the
  remote's history deleted it. So the one decision is: keep that synthetic
  sample in tests/, or drop it too.

  Since you run git, here are the two safe resolutions — pick one, then commit +
  push:

  Keep it (matches your earlier "track data/out/tests/ for review transparency"
  decision; it's a tiny, regenerable sample) — my recommendation:
  git add data/out/tests/P000.json
  git commit            # completes the merge
  git push

  Drop it entirely (no synthetic P000 anywhere):
  git rm data/out/tests/P000.json
  git commit
  git push

  ▎ ★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────
  ▎ Why git couldn't auto-resolve: to git this is a rename/delete — your side 
  ▎ moved data/out/P000.json → data/out/tests/P000.json (a rename + content 
  ▎ tweak), while the remote deleted the original. Git won't assume a deletion 
  ▎ should "follow" a rename, so it surfaces it as deleted by them on the new 
  ▎ path and asks you. git add = "keep my renamed version", git rm = "honor 
  ▎ their delete". Either way the original data/out/P000.json stays deleted, 
  ▎ which is what you asked for.
  ▎ ─────────────────────────────────────────────────

  Don't run git merge --abort unless you want to discard the pull entirely. Once
  you've committed the merge and pushed, say go and I'll start the scaled-up
  build at step 1 (reading data/in/interviews.json).

⏺ Running the export and confirming fire made it in (and the gate goes green now

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ The export ran, but it surfaced the real blocker: fire isn't in the lockfile 
  yet, so requirements.txt is unchanged and the gate still shows the one ty
  diagnostic (import fire unresolved in main.py). uv export only mirrors uv.lock
  — it can't add a dependency that was never installed.

  So the order needs to be:
  1. uv add fire ← this is the missing step (installs it + writes to
  pyproject.toml + uv.lock).
  2. uv export --format requirements.txt --output-file requirements.txt ← then
  it picks up fire.

  You run dep-adds, so once you've done uv add fire, ping me and I'll re-run the
  export (or you can — it's the command above). At that point the gate goes
  green: ruff and pytest already pass (4 passed, 1 skipped), and ty's lone
  diagnostic is purely the unresolved fire import.

  Everything else this round is in place:
  - extract.py → clean single call, now with a model= param and the JSON-format
  line in SYSTEM_PROMPT.
  - main.py → load_interviews + run loop + main fire CLI (--model, --limit).
  - docs/DESIGN.qmd (Mermaid draft) + TODO Reporting section (README.qmd
  answers, DESIGN.qmd finalisation).

  ▎ ★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────
  ▎ Why the gate flags fire but not at runtime-only: ty does static import 
  ▎ resolution across src/, so an unresolved import is a type error even though 
  ▎ pytest never imports main.py (it's outside testpaths). That's actually the 
  ▎ gate doing its job — catching a missing dependency before you try to run the
  ▎ CLI, rather than at python src/main.py time. The same separation we set up 
  ▎ earlier (ty scans src, pytest only tests) is what makes this a clean "1 
  ▎ diagnostic" rather than a test crash.
  ▎ ─────────────────────────────────────────────────

⏺ fire is added now (pyproject shows fire>=0.7.1), and your CLI test exposed the
  real problem: even though extract.py logged the clean 503 line, the exception
  then propagated up through run → main → fire, dumping a wall of traceback.
  That's the per-record resilience we flagged — now it bites at the CLI.

  The fix is per-record handling in the loop (main.run), where we agreed it
  belongs: catch each failure, log it, skip and continue, and report a summary —
  so one 503 never aborts the batch or dumps a traceback. Editing main.py:

❯ i ran 3 but it really should have failed gracefully on the first and exited 

⏺ Ah — clearer now: you want fail-fast graceful exit on the first 503, not
  skip-and-continue. That's the right call for a model-wide 503 (if the model's
  busy, records 2 and 3 will 503 too — no point grinding through them). So I'll
  catch the ServiceUnavailableError at the CLI boundary and exit cleanly with a
  message, no traceback. (Reverting the per-record logger import I'd started —
  wrong approach for this.)

⏺ Very useful failure — and note what it actually is: a 429 daily-quota 
  exhaustion (limit: 20 … GenerateRequestsPerDayPerProjectPerModel-FreeTier).
  Two things follow:

  1. Code: handle RateLimitError like the 503 — back off + retry (via litellm's
  num_retries), then fail gracefully after the retries. Right now main only
  catches ServiceUnavailableError, so the 429 dumped the traceback.
  2. Scaling reality: the free tier is 20 requests/day — 50 patients can't
  complete in one day on it. That's a real constraint (paid tier, or batch
  across days) worth flagging.

  Implementing: a RETRYABLE tuple (429 + 503), num_retries on the call so
  litellm backs off + retries, a clean log line, and the CLI catching both →
  graceful exit. extract.py first:

⏺ Thanks — json confirmed fixed (line 10). Now the compatibility: the key gap is
  that our response_format uses Gemini-specific keys (response_schema,
  enforce_validation) that Ollama doesn't accept. The clean litellm way to make
  one call work across both providers is drop_params=True — litellm drops
  whatever the target provider doesn't support (Gemini keeps them; Ollama falls
  back to JSON mode), so the --model swap "just works."

  Adding drop_params, an Ollama CLI example, and the local-model note in
  SETUP.md:

⏺ Gate green. The model swap is wired and provider-compatible:

  - drop_params=True on the litellm.completion call — the same call works for
  both: Gemini keeps response_schema/enforce_validation; Ollama drops them and
  falls back to JSON mode. No branching in extract().
  - --model=ollama_chat/<model> swaps to the local model (documented in
  main.py's CLI examples and SETUP.md's rate-limit section as the quota
  workaround).
  - The 429/503 retry + graceful exit from before is intact.

  To try it once gemma3 finishes downloading (you have ollama installed):
  ollama serve            # in one terminal
  ollama pull gemma3      # if not already
  uv run python src/main.py --model=ollama_chat/gemma3 --limit=3

  ▎ ★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────
  ▎ drop_params is litellm's portability lever: you send one superset of params 
  ▎ and it strips whatever the target provider rejects. But note the asymmetry 
  ▎ it papers over — Gemini enforces the schema server-side (responseJsonSchema 
  ▎ + enforce_validation), whereas Ollama only gets plain JSON mode, so a small 
  ▎ local model isn't constrained to your schema, just nudged by the 
  ▎ SYSTEM_PROMPT's "return valid JSON matching the schema." That makes 
  ▎ PatientLabels.model_validate_json(...) the common validation floor across 
  ▎ providers — and you should expect a small gemma3 to fail that validation 
  ▎ more often than Gemini did. That's exactly the experiment: can a local model
  ▎ produce schema-valid output well enough to dodge the quota?
  ▎ ─────────────────────────────────────────────────

  I couldn't live-test the Ollama path (server's not up / model still
  downloading on your machine), so that verification is yours. Everything else

⏺ Right — a wrong/unpulled model surfaces as litellm.APIConnectionError ("model
  'gemma3' not found"), which main doesn't catch (only RETRYABLE), so it dumped
  the wall again (plus litellm futilely retried it 2×). Let me fold
  connection/model-not-found errors into the graceful handling. I'll generalise
  RETRYABLE → HANDLED (adds APIConnectionError) so it's logged cleanly, excluded
  from @logger.catch's dump, and caught at the CLI:

⏺ Update(src/extract.py)
  ⎿  Added 7 lines, removed 1 line
      29  
      30  MODEL = "gemini/gemini-2.5-flash-lite"
      31  MAX_RETRIES = 2  # litellm retries transient 429/503 errors, wi
          th backoff
      32 -RETRYABLE = (litellm.RateLimitError, litellm.ServiceUnavailable
         -Error)  # 429 / 503                                            
      32 +# API errors we log cleanly and surface for a graceful CLI exit
         + (no traceback dump):                                          
      33 +# 429 (rate limit), 503 (model busy), and connection / model-no
         +t-found.                                                       
      34 +HANDLED = (                                                    
      35 +    litellm.RateLimitError,                                    
      36 +    litellm.ServiceUnavailableError,                           
      37 +    litellm.APIConnectionError,                                
      38 +)                                                              
      39  # Anchor paths to the repo root via __file__, so they hold rega
          rdless of caller's cwd
      40  # (e.g. the notebook runs from notebooks/, not the repo root).
      41  ROOT = Path(__file__).resolve().parents[1]

            2026")

⏺ Here's what the research surfaces — and importantly, model choice is only half
  the problem; the bigger lever is the response_format form for Ollama.

  Candidate models (M1 Pro 64GB, via ollama_chat/<model>)

  Model: Qwen3 (esp. 30B‑A3B MoE)
  ~Size / speed on 64GB: fits unified memory, ~30 tok/s on M1 Max (slower on M1
    Pro, still usable)
  Why: Repeatedly cited as best for structured output / JSON / tool-use; MoE =
    big-model quality at fast speed (~3B active)
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Model: Qwen3 8B / Qwen2.5‑7B‑instruct
  ~Size / speed on 64GB: small, fast
  Why: Closest "speed equivalent" to gemini‑2.5‑flash‑lite
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Model: Llama 3.1 8B / 3.3 70B
  ~Size / speed on 64GB: 8B fast; 70B fits quantized but slow on M1 Pro
  Why: Solid structured output
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Model: Gemma 4 26B‑A4B (MoE)
  ~Size / speed on 64GB: fits
  Why: A bigger Gemma than the one that failed — MoE alternative

  For your "flash-lite equivalent" sweet spot: Qwen3 30B‑A3B (quality + MoE
  speed) or qwen3:8b (fastest). Qwen is the consistent winner for schema
  adherence.

  The actual compatibility blocker (why gemma4 invented fields)

  Ollama can do near‑100% schema adherence — but only when the schema is passed
  as response_format={"type": "json_schema", "json_schema": {...}} (constrains
  inference). Our call uses Gemini's {"type": "json_object", "response_schema": 
  …}, and with drop_params=True Ollama just gets basic JSON mode — not
  schema‑constrained. So the model free-forms its own fields, and only
  enforce_validation catches it afterward. Even Qwen3 will do this until
  extract() sends Ollama the json_schema form.

  ▎ ★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────
  ▎ So the gemma4 failure was two problems stacked: a weak small model and an 
  ▎ unconstrained call. A bigger model (Qwen3) fixes the first; provider-aware 
  ▎ response_format fixes the second. The second matters more — without 
  ▎ schema-constrained decoding, you're relying on prompt-adherence, which is 
  ▎ exactly what small local models are worst at. The clean fix is to branch 
  ▎ response_format on model.startswith("ollama") → Ollama gets json_schema, 
  ▎ Gemini keeps response_schema.
  ▎ ─────────────────────────────────────────────────

  Sources:
  - Structured outputs · Ollama Blog · Ollama structured-outputs docs
  - Constraining LLMs with Structured Output: Ollama, Qwen3 (Glukhov)
  - MacBook Pro M1 Max 64GB: Best Local LLMs (willitrunai) · Best Local LLMs for
  Mac 2026 (InsiderLLM)

  Want me to (a) add a TODO capturing this (Qwen3 candidate + the json_schema
  provider-branch), and/or (b) wire the provider-aware response_format now so an
  Ollama model is actually schema-constrained?

❯ i'm going for qwen3:8b  due to instruction/speed tradeoff                     
  ⎿  ⧉ Selected 1 lines from src/extract.py in Visual Studio Code

⏺ Good pick — qwen3:8b is the right instruction/speed tradeoff:
  instruction-tuned Qwen follows schemas far more reliably than gemma, and 8B
  keeps it fast on the M1 Pro.

  One expectation to set with the current code: because drop_params=True strips
  the Gemini schema params, Ollama only gets basic JSON mode — so qwen3:8b is
  prompt-guided, not decoding-constrained to PatientLabels. It'll likely do much
  better than gemma4, but if it free-forms its own fields you'll now get the
  graceful JSONSchemaValidationError exit (not the traceback wall). To try it:

  ollama pull qwen3:8b
  uv run python src/main.py --model=ollama_chat/qwen3:8b --limit=3

  ▎ ★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────
  ▎ The thing to watch: qwen3:8b passing the run only tells you it followed the 
  ▎ prompt well enough this time — it's not the same guarantee as Gemini's 
  ▎ server-side responseJsonSchema, which constrains the decoder so invalid 
  ▎ tokens literally can't be emitted. For a small model that distinction is 
  ▎ where reliability lives. If a few of the 50 fail validation, that's the gap 
  ▎ — not a reason to abandon the model.
  ▎ ─────────────────────────────────────────────────

  If qwen3:8b proves flaky on the schema, the fix is the provider-aware
  response_format (send Ollama {"type": "json_schema", "json_schema": …} so its
  decoder is constrained too). Whenever you want, I can:
  - (a) wire that provider-branch + set qwen3:8b as the default OLLAMA_MODEL in
  the test/docstring, and
  - (b) drop a TODO capturing the Qwen3 choice + the json_schema branch.

  Try it first — then tell me what you saw and we'll decide if (a) is needed.

✻ Worked for 1m 4s

❯ we can scale up to 30B if necessary                                           

⏺ Exactly — and the nice part is scaling up is just a flag, no code change:
  --model=ollama_chat/qwen3:30b-a3b (the MoE variant) once it's pulled. The
  30B‑A3B fits 64GB comfortably and, being MoE (~3B active), stays usable on the
  M1 Pro rather than crawling like a dense 30B would.

  The decision tree, then:
  1. qwen3:8b — fast; see how many of the 50 pass schema validation.
  2. qwen3:30b-a3b — flag swap if 8B free-forms too often; bigger → better
  prompt-schema adherence, fewer validation failures.
  3. json_schema response_format branch — the only thing that guarantees
  adherence regardless of model, since it constrains Ollama's decoder (matching
  what Gemini does server-side).

  ▎ ★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────
  ▎ Worth being clear on what scaling buys you: 8B → 30B shrinks the failure 
  ▎ rate (a smarter model follows the prompt's schema description more often), 
  ▎ but it doesn't change the failure mode — both are prompt-guided, so neither 
  ▎ is impossible-to-violate the way Gemini's constrained decoding is. So scale 
  ▎ up for accuracy on the content of the labels; reach for the json_schema 
  ▎ branch when you want the structure to be guaranteed. They're solving 
  ▎ different halves.
  ▎ ─────────────────────────────────────────────────

  Nothing to change right now — the --model swap already covers 8B↔30B. Run
  qwen3:8b, and tell me the pass/fail split across the slice; that'll tell us
  whether to bump to 30B, add the json_schema branch, or both.

  Two structural problems for Q3 in the current schema:
  1. No before/after marker on TreatmentRecord — the split is only inferable
  from list order.
  2. treatment_class is inconsistent free text ("conventional" vs
  "conventional_therapy"), and the biologic often isn't a record at all
  (prescribed-but-not-taken). So a post-hoc EDA split is unreliable.

  ▎ ★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────
  ▎ P002 is the decisive case: Q3 is "treatments tried before a biologic is 
  ▎ considered", and for a patient who never started one, the answer is "all of 
  ▎ them" — but a naive "split the list at the biologic" returns nothing, 
  ▎ because there's no biologic row to split on. The robust fix is to let the 
  ▎ model (which has the full narrative) mark each treatment, rather than 
  ▎ reconstruct timing from an unordered, free-text list downstream. One 
  ▎ explicit boolean beats fragile inference.
  ▎ ─────────────────────────────────────────────────

  TreatmentRecord + one prompt line. Then the 3-case run also validates that Q3
  field. Making the change:

  The one blocker (must fix before the run)

  churn/incomplete_journey came back null in 2 of 3 cases (P001, P002 both null;
  P003 happened to get churn=true). The cause: the SYSTEM_PROMPT gives zero 
  guidance on them, so the model usually skips them. The entire section-5 churn
  analysis — "how many truncated vs complete," the 3-way split, "which answers
  are least trustworthy given churn" — depends on this signal being populated.
  Running 50 without fixing it wastes the run for that analysis.

  ▎ ★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────
  ▎ This is the inverse of the Q3 fix: there, the model had the data but we 
  ▎ needed a field to capture it; here, the field exists but the model needs 
  ▎ instruction to fill it. Both are "ensure the answer is in the structured 
  ▎ output, not reconstructable-only-by-luck." The reason it's a genuine 
  ▎ question and not just a prompt tweak is that churn is the one definition 
  ▎ you've repeatedly (and rightly) reserved — and the README's hint ("disengage
  ▎ partway… truncated journeys") overlaps it with incomplete_journey, so how 
  ▎ to populate the two is your call.

  ① Reviewed docs/TASK_INSTRUCTIONS.md — it confirms your churn reading: line 21
  ("disengage partway… truncated journeys") and line 42 ("varying completeness…

  ③ README ### Churn — definition & handling — concise justification of the
  app-interaction reading, the one-flag decision, the (a)/(b) operational
  definition, and how it powers the three-way analysis split.

  ▎ ★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────
  ▎ The "third person → talking to an app, not a doctor" inference is the 
  ▎ load-bearing move: it's what lets churn = app-disengagement = transcript 
  ▎ truncation collapse into one signal. Without that framing, churn (clinical 
  ▎ disengagement) and incomplete_journey (truncated record) are genuinely 
  ▎ different axes and you'd be stuck maintaining two noisy flags. The decision 
  ▎ trades a sliver of theoretical precision for a definition the model can 
  ▎ actually apply consistently — which is exactly what the spec rewards 
  ▎ ("first-class signals, not silently collapse into nulls").
  ▎ ─────────────────────────────────────────────────

  ▎ ★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────
  ▎ Externalizing the prompt to data/prompts/system.txt is a small but real 
  ▎ architecture win: the prompt is now data, not code — it diffs cleanly in 
  ▎ review, can be iterated without touching extract.py, and (down the line) 
  ▎ makes the "split into concurrent prompts" future step a matter of adding 
  ▎ treatment.txt, funnel.txt, etc. alongside it rather than refactoring Python.
  ▎ The one tradeoff: it's read at import time, so a missing/renamed file fails
  ▎ loudly on import — which is the behavior you want for a required asset.
  ▎ ─────────────────────────────────────────────────

⏺ Done — synced cleanly and verified. The notebook now has an explicit "What 
  gets sent to Gemini" phase between the imports and the extraction-return cell.

  New inspect cell prints, in order:
  1. System prompt — the exact SYSTEM_PROMPT loaded from data/prompts/system.txt
  (1010 chars, churn guidance and all).
  2. User message — patient_id: P000\n\n<transcript>, the real message shape.
  3. response_format.response_schema — PatientLabels.model_json_schema()
  pretty-printed: the full $defs/properties/required JSON Schema that constrains
  Gemini's output.

  Then the existing run cell makes the live call and prints the returned
  PatientLabels JSON — so you see inputs → schema → output in sequence.

  ▎ ★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────
  ▎ The cell deliberately mirrors the messages/response_format that extract() 
  ▎ builds rather than importing them — extract.py constructs that payload 
  ▎ inline inside the function, so there's nothing to import. The tradeoff: it's
  ▎ a small duplication that could drift if extract()'s payload changes (hence 
  ▎ the # Same shape extract() sends (see src/extract.py) comment). The 
  ▎ alternative — refactoring extract() to expose a build_payload() helper — 
  ▎ would be cleaner for reuse but is scope you didn't ask for. For an 
  ▎ inspection notebook, the mirror is the lighter call. Also note this reads 
  ▎ the schema/prompt without an API call, so the inspect cell is free to run; 
  ▎ only the final cell spends a token.
  ▎ ─────────────────────────────────────────────────

  The .py is the source of truth and the .ipynb was updated with --update
  (existing outputs preserved). Run the inspect cell then the run cell in VS
  Code to see it live. Nothing in the gate's scope changed. Want me to also drop
  incomplete_journey from src/schema.py next (so the printed schema matches
  v4), or leave that for its own step?

  ▎ ★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────
  ▎ Worth remembering the ordering invariant here: uv export only ever 
  ▎ serializes what's already in uv.lock — it can't discover a dependency. So 
  ▎ the reliable sequence is always uv add <pkg> (updates pyproject.toml + 
  ▎ uv.lock) → uv export (mirrors lock → requirements.txt). Since you'd already 
  ▎ uv add-ed great-tables/plotnine earlier, the lock was current and this 
  ▎ export just had catching-up to do. If requirements.txt ever looks stale, the
  ▎ question is whether the lock was updated, not whether export ran.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Diagnosed — with evidence from the actual files. Here's what's happening.

  Why the model input/output aren't in a datetime-stamped log

  Root cause: print() ≠ logging. The inspect cell's print(...) (system prompt,
  schema, user message) and the run cell's print(labels.model_dump_json()) write
  to the notebook's cell output (stdout) — nothing routes stdout to a file. So
  those values never reach logs/.

  What each log does capture, from the evidence:

  Where: logs/extract.log (loguru)
  Has the input/output?: ❌ only the terse line: …09:10:32 … extracted P000 -> 
    data/out/tests/P000.json
  Datetime stamp?: ✅ full YYYY-MM-DD HH:MM:SS
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Where: logs/litellm_debug.log (stdlib)
  Has the input/output?: ✅ the full request (prompt+schema+user msg) and RAW 
    RESPONSE (output JSON) — modified 09:10, so your run is in it
  Datetime stamp?: ❌ none — grep for YYYY-MM-DD found nothing
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Where: data/out/tests/P000.json
  Has the input/output?: ✅ the output JSON (file mtime ≈ datetime)
  Datetime stamp?: (file timestamp only)

  So the input+output are persisted (in litellm_debug.log + the output file) —
  but the one file with a real datetime stamp (extract.log) only records the
  one-line outcome, and the verbose file that has the content has no date.

  Why litellm_debug.log has no datetime: the notebook attaches a bare
  logging.FileHandler(...) with no formatter, so it writes LiteLLM's raw
  messages — which carry LiteLLM's own HH:MM:SS prefix (time-only) plus ANSI
  colour codes, but no date. A FileHandler adds nothing unless you give it a
  Formatter.

  ▎ ★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────
  ▎ Two independent logging systems, two different gaps. loguru (extract.log) 
  ▎ datetime-stamps automatically but we only ever call logger.info("extracted 
  ▎ …") — terse by design. The stdlib LiteLLM logger carries the rich payload 
  ▎ but a FileHandler() with no Formatter is a dumb pipe: it writes 
  ▎ record.getMessage() verbatim, so you inherit LiteLLM's time-only prefix. The
  ▎ fix isn't "log more" — the content is already there — it's "stamp the 
  ▎ handler that has it."
  ▎ ─────────────────────────────────────────────────

  The fix (your choice)

  - Minimal — stamp the verbose stream: give the notebook's FileHandler a
  logging.Formatter("%(asctime)s | %(message)s") so every request + RAW RESPONSE
  line in litellm_debug.log gets a full datetime (optionally strip ANSI for
  readability).
  - Cleaner — a structured per-run record: add a logger.info in extract() (or
  the notebook) that writes the user message + returned JSON to extract.log, so
  the datetime-stamped log itself carries input→output (at some verbosity cost).

  I'd go minimal (formatter on the handler) since the content is already
  captured there. Want me to apply that?

  One thing the pasted schema reveals (you said "confirm the model is receiving
  the correct input"): the schema still contains incomplete_journey alongside
  churn — we merged that away in v4 but haven't yet dropped it from
  src/schema.py, so the model is still being sent the retired field. Worth doing
  before the 50-run so the input matches v4.

      199  ## Changelog
      200  
      201 +- **v0.4** — **`incomplete_journey` merged into `churn` and dr
          +opped** (working file `_v4.ods`).                             
      202 +  *Why:* reading the "interview" as the patient's interaction 
          +with the Mama Health app, `churn`                             
      203 +  (app disengagement) and `incomplete_journey` (truncated tran
          +script) are the **same signal**, so

          +n"). `churn` is therefore defined                             
      205 +  (interview truncated / patient disengaged), no longer "pendi
          +ng CAIO". `TreatmentRecord` gained                            
      206 +  `before_biologic` (the before/after split for Q3). `PatientL
          +abels` is now implemented in

          +stem.txt`.

⏺ Update(docs/QUESTIONS.md)
  ⎿  Added 8 lines, removed 1 line
      19  **Spec hint (README):** *"Some patients disengage partway throu
          gh their interview — they churn —
      20  leaving us with truncated journeys."* This frames churn as **di
          sengaging partway through the
      21  interview itself** (a truncated transcript) — closer to our `in
          complete_journey` notion (Q4) than
      22 -to app/treatment churn, so the two definitions still need recon
         -ciling.                                                        
      22 +to app/treatment churn.                                        
      23  
      24 +**Decision (v4):** we read the "interview" as the patient's int
         +eraction with the Mama Health app                              
      25 +(patients describe their doctors in the *third person*, so they
         +'re narrating to the app, not a                                
      26 +clinician). So `churn` = disengagement from that interaction, w
         +hich surfaces as a **truncated                                 
      27 +transcript**. Because that is the *same* signal as `incomplete_
         +journey`, the two are **merged into

  ▎ ★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────
  ▎ The audit's real payoff is the category split: a grep hit isn't 
  ▎ automatically a problem. Here the same token "incomplete_journey" appears as
  ▎ (a) a retired model field — the one that had to go, (b) explanatory prose 
  ▎ about its removal — which should stay, and (c) frozen changelog history — 
  ▎ which must not change. Conflating those would either leave a live bug or 
  ▎ vandalize the record. "Dropped from the pipeline" means dropped from the 
  ▎ executable path (schema → prompt → extract → tests), which is now verified —
  ▎ not erased from the documentation that explains the decision.

⏺ Diagnosis confirmed with hard evidence:

  The prompt/schema in extract.log are incidental crash residue, not deliberate 
  logging. Lines 174 & 337 (│ └ {'model': …, 'messages': [{'role': 'system'…)
  are loguru @logger.catch traceback variable-dumps from the failed runs on 
  2026-06-19 (the 503s and the gemma JSONSchemaValidationError). The only
  deliberate INFO logging is lines 1014/1017 — P000 input: … (just the user 
  message/transcript) and P000 output: ….

  So: extract.py never logs the system prompt or the schema. It logs the
  transcript as "input" + the output JSON. The full prompt/schema blocks you see
  come only from the notebook inspect cell's print() → notebook stdout, never
  the log.

⏺ You're right, and I was imprecise — let me explain exactly what I meant,
  because it hinges on a distinction between two different reason fields.

  There are two reason enums in the schema:

  Field: reasons_for_biologic_prescribed
  Enum: ReasonPrescribed
  Answers: why a biologic was the chosen path
  Values: DOCTOR_CHOICE, PATIENT_FEARS, COST, ACCESS, NOT_APPLICABLE, OTHER
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Field: reasons_for_biologic_not_taken
  Enum: ReasonNotTaken
  Answers: why a prescribed biologic wasn't taken
  Values: NOT_MENTIONED, EXPLICIT_DENIAL, INSURANCE_PROBLEMS, COST,
    PATIENT_FEARS, CONTRAINDICATION, DEFERRED, JOURNEY_CUT_OFF, UNKNOWN,
    NOT_APPLICABLE, OTHER

  So DOCTOR_CHOICE/ACCESS do exist — in ReasonPrescribed. My table line was
  sloppy: I should have written "no first-class DOCTOR_CHOICE/ACCESS in 
  ReasonNotTaken."

  Why that distinction matters for Q2: Q2 asks about patients not on a biologic.
  For them, reasons_for_biologic_prescribed is NOT_APPLICABLE (or describes why
  it was offered, not why they're not on it) — so it's not the field that 
  answers Q2. The Q2 field is reasons_for_biologic_not_taken (+
  biologic_not_mentioned), and that enum is the one without literal
  DOCTOR_CHOICE/ACCESS.

  But — reconsidering, this isn't actually a gap. I overcalled the ⚠️. The
  README's example words map cleanly onto what we persist:
  - "patient fears" → PATIENT_FEARS
  - "cost" → COST
  - "access" → INSURANCE_PROBLEMS (the dominant access barrier in the data) /
  COST
  - "doctor choice" → biologic_not_mentioned=true (the clinician never escalated
  — managing on conventional therapy) or DEFERRED/CONTRAINDICATION (clinical
  reasons)
  - "something else" → OTHER + evidence_notes

  ▎ ★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────
  ▎ The schema deliberately splits "why prescribed" from "why not taken" because
  ▎ they're different events in the biologic funnel — and DOCTOR_CHOICE is 
  ▎ asymmetric: a doctor choosing a biologic is a real reason it was prescribed,
  ▎ but a patient not on one rarely has "doctor chose against it" stated as a 
  ▎ reason — it shows up as the biologic simply never being raised 
  ▎ (biologic_not_mentioned). So the minimal, honest mapping is to read "doctor 
  ▎ choice" off biologic_not_mentioned, not to bolt a DOCTOR_CHOICE onto 
  ▎ ReasonNotTaken. That keeps the enum matched to what transcripts actually 
  ▎ say.
  ▎ ─────────────────────────────────────────────────

  Revised verdict: Q2 needs no schema change — answerable from
  reasons_for_biologic_not_taken + biologic_not_mentioned, with the tradeoff (we
  infer "doctor choice" from "biologic never raised") noted in the README. So
  gap 3 from my audit is withdrawn.

  logging (one logger.info of response.usage + cost so the 50-run is
  telemetry-complete). Shall I add the telemetry log line before you re-run the
  single example?

  On the size/latency vs instruction-following tradeoff

  Model (pulled): qwen3:8b
  Size: 5.2 GB
  Latency: fast

  Verdict: too weak
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Model (pulled): gemma4

  Instruction-following: invented its own fields

  ────────────────────────────────────────

  Instruction-following: best of the three

  ▎ ★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────
  ▎ The cold-start is the trap, not the model size. Ollama loads the full 18 GB 
  ▎ and spins the GPU on the first call (the fan you heard), then keeps it warm 
  ▎ for ~5 min — subsequent calls are fast. So the iteration recipe is: one slow
  ▎ warm-up call, then iterate quickly within the keep-alive window 
  ▎ (OLLAMA_KEEP_ALIVE=30m extends it). qwen3:30b-a3b is the realistic floor — 
  ▎ the 8B/gemma4 are fast but don't follow the schema, which defeats the point.
  ▎ The open risk: even 30B via Ollama gets plain JSON mode, not Gemini's 
  ▎ schema-constrained decoding — so your 1-call test is what tells us if it 
  ▎ actually adheres. If it free-forms like the 8B did, that's the signal to 
  ▎ stop and use chunked Gemini.
  ▎ ─────────────────────────────────────────────────

  Chunked Gemini — feasibility (the parallel track)

  Measured: 671 tokens/patient (269 in + 402 out). For a 10-patient chunk: ~850
  in + ~4,000 out ≈ 4.9K tokens/call — well under the 250K TPM, and output ~4K <
  the ~8K default cap ✓. So 10×5 = 5 requests < 20/day, and you have ~15 calls 
  left today → it fits comfortably now.

  The build is a focused --chunk-size CLI feature: a BatchPredictions wrapper
  schema (predictions: list[PatientLabels]), an extract_batch() that sends N
  transcripts in one call and persists/logs each, reusing all the per-step
  logging. I'd not run it blind (saves your quota) — build it, you test with 1
  chunk.

⏺ Here's the scope for the --chunk-size chunked-Gemini feature, grounded in the
  current extract.py / main.py / schema.py. No code yet — this is the spec for
  you to approve.

  Chunked extraction — feature scope

  Goal: send N transcripts per Gemini call so the 50-run fits the
  20-requests/day cap (10×5 = 5 calls), reusing the existing per-step
  logging/telemetry.

  Files touched (small, additive — nothing existing changes behaviour)

  ┌───────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │         File          │                      Change                      │
  ├───────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │                       │ + class BatchPredictions(BaseModel):             │
  │ src/schema.py         │ predictions: list[PatientLabels] (object-root    │
  │                       │ wrapper — Gemini handles $defs/$ref, per the PR  │
  │                       │ we documented in RESOURCES.md)                   │
  ├───────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │                       │ + extract_batch(interviews, out_dir, model) ->   │
  │ src/extract.py        │ list[PatientLabels] — one call for a chunk;      │
  │                       │ reuses save(), HANDLED, MAX_RETRIES, and the     │
  │                       │ input/raw/telemetry/output logging               │
  ├───────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │                       │ + run_chunked(records, model, out_dir,           │
  │ src/main.py           │ chunk_size); main() gains chunk_size: int | None │
  │                       │  = None (None → current per-call path)           │
  ├───────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ data/prompts/         │ batch framing (see decision ① below)             │
  ├───────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ tests/test_extract.py │ + offline BatchPredictions round-trip; + gated   │
  │                       │ live 2-record batch                              │
  └───────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  How extract_batch works

  1. Build one user message: each transcript prefixed patient_id: P0NN,
  delimited (---).
  2. response_format uses BatchPredictions.model_json_schema(); set
  max_tokens=8192 (headroom — see ③).
  3. Log input (batched message) → raw response → telemetry (response.usage, now
  chunk totals) — same pattern as extract().
  4. BatchPredictions.model_validate_json(raw) → loop predictions: save() +
  per-patient output log → return list.

  Design decisions & tradeoffs (your calls)

  ① Batch prompt. The field guidance in system.txt is identical per patient.
  Options: a separate system_batch.txt (consistent with externalizing prompts,
  but duplicates the guidance) vs reuse system.txt + a small in-code
  BATCH_PREAMBLE ("you'll get several interviews each prefixed by patient_id;
  return one prediction per patient, copy the id exactly"). Recommend the 
  preamble — the batch text is call-mechanics, not field semantics, so it's
  DRYer.

  ② Failure granularity (the big one). A chunk is all-or-nothing: if the model
  returns the wrong count, malformed JSON, or one bad record, the chunk's
  validation fails → lose all 10. Two levels:
  - Strict (minimal): BatchPredictions.model_validate_json — simplest, but 1 bad
  record kills the chunk.
  - Tolerant (more code): parse the JSON array, validate each item
  independently, persist the good ones, log which patient_ids failed + a count
  check (len(predictions) == len(chunk)).
  - Recommend tolerant — it preserves the per-record resilience you valued, and
  keeps the "diagnose which field/patient failed" property. Small extra parse
  loop.

  ③ Chunk-size ceiling. Output ≈ chunk_size × ~400 tokens. Default cap ~8K →
  keep chunk_size ≤ ~15. Default 10 (~4K out, safe). I'll log() if a chunk is
  dropped, never silently truncate.

  ④ Quality tradeoff. The model attends to 10 transcripts at once → per-patient
  extraction (esp. before_biologic, referral_pathway) may be slightly less
  careful than a dedicated call. The golden-set eval will quantify it; worth a
  README caveat.

  Feasibility (measured)

  671 tok/patient → 10-chunk ≈ 4.9K tokens/call (≪ 250K TPM, ~4K out < 8K cap).
  10×5 = 5 requests < 20/day, fits in your ~15 remaining today.

  ▎ ★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────
  ▎ The whole feature is additive — extract() stays the canonical 1-patient path
  ▎ (and the test/eval gold standard), extract_batch() is a sibling that trades
  ▎ per-patient isolation for request efficiency. That's deliberate: if batch 
  ▎ quality disappoints on the golden set, you flip back to per-call without 
  ▎ unwinding anything. The one property you genuinely give up is Gemini's 
  ▎ schema-constrained decoding per record — the wrapper still validates, but a 
  ▎ 10-record generation has more room to drift than ten 1-record generations. 
  ▎ The tolerant parse (②) is what keeps that from being all-or-nothing.
  ▎ ─────────────────────────────────────────────────

  Effort: ~1 wrapper model + 1 function + 1 CLI branch + 2 tests. I'd build it
  without running it (saves quota); you test one chunk via --chunk-size=10 
  --limit=10 --out-dir=data/out/tests.

  Want me to add this to docs/TODO.md and build it now (so it's ready while you
  test qwen), or adjust the scope first — particularly decisions ①/②?

  ▎ ★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────
  ▎ This is why import schema said "OK" but the build failed: Python 3.14 
  ▎ evaluates annotations lazily (PEP 649), so ReasonPrescribed | None isn't 
  ▎ resolved at class-definition time — the import sails through. Pydantic only 
  ▎ forces resolution when it builds the core schema (model_json_schema() / 
  ▎ first validation), which is where the NameError-equivalent surfaces. A 
  ▎ missing type is now a latent break that hides until the model is actually 
  ▎ used — exactly the trap here.
  ▎ ─────────────────────────────────────────────────

⏺ Gate green. The ollama tests now degrade gracefully instead of dumping that
  traceback:

  Test: test_ollama_server_running
  Failure mode: ollama serve down (httpx.RequestError)
  Now reports: skip — "not reachable, run ollama serve"
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Test: test_ollama_extract_schema
  Failure mode: litellm.APIConnectionError (model not pulled)
  Now reports: skip — "unreachable / not pulled"
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Test: test_ollama_extract_schema
  Failure mode: litellm.JSONSchemaValidationError (model invents fields)
  Now reports: xfail — one line + first 300 chars of the raw output

  So your next RUN_OLLAMA_TESTS=1 uv run pytest tests/test_ollama.py -v will
  show XFAIL with a concise reason like "qwen3:30b-a3b returned schema-invalid 
  output: {"patient_id":"P000","churn":false,"treatment_records":[{"treatment":"
  mesalamine"…" — not 500 lines.

  ▎ ★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────
  ▎ The semantic choice matters here: xfail, not skip, for the schema failure. A
  ▎ skip says "didn't run"; an xfail says "ran, failed as we expected it might"
  ▎ — which is the honest result for a capability probe of a local model. And 
  ▎ crucially it's not a red FAILED, so the suite stays green while still 
  ▎ recording that qwen3:30b-a3b can't hold this schema. The 300-char 
  ▎ raw_response slice is the diagnostic: from your run it shows exactly why — 
  ▎ treatment instead of name, no required biologic_* fields, free-text 
  ▎ referral_pathway ("diagnosis", "first_line_treatment"). That's the 
  ▎ local-model verdict in one line: it won't comply without schema-constrained 
  ▎ decoding, which Ollama's plain JSON mode (what drop_params leaves it) 
  ▎ doesn't give.
  ▎ ─────────────────────────────────────────────────

  This confirms your prediction empirically: local qwen is out for this pipeline
  as-is (non-compliant and ~2.5 min/call). That makes chunked Gemini the path
  to actually complete the 50. The chunking scope is approved and ready — shall
  I build it now (commit this test-handling first)? And the still-open thread:
  the e.raw_response logging fix in extract.py (so a schema failure logs the
  model's raw output to extract.log too, not just the test).

      131 +BATCH_SUFFIX = (                                              
      132 +    " You are given several interviews, each prefixed with its
          + patient_id and separated by '---'. "

          +ng each patient_id exactly."

      137 +@logger.catch(exclude=HANDLED, reraise=True)                  
      138 +def extract_batch(                                            
      139 +    interviews: list[Interview], out_dir: Path = OUT_DIR, mode
          +l: str = MODEL                                                
      140 +) -> list[PatientLabels]:                                     
      141 +    """Extract a chunk of interviews in ONE call — fewer reque
          +sts for the daily cap (see main.py                            
      142 +    `--chunk-size`). Strict: a malformed/short batch fails the
          + whole chunk (minimal; a tolerant                             
      143 +    per-record parse is future hardening — docs/TODO.md)."""  
      144 +    load_dotenv()  # GEMINI_API_KEY -> env                    
      145 +    ids = [r.patient_id for r in interviews]                  
      146 +    user_message = "\n\n---\n\n".join(                        
      147 +        f"patient_id: {r.patient_id}\n\n{r.interview_transcrip
          +t}" for r in interviews                                       
      148 +    )                                                         
      149 +    logger.info("batch input ({} patients): {}", len(ids), ids
          +)                                                             
      150 +    try:                                                      
      151 +        response = litellm.completion(                        
      152 +            model=model,                                      
      153 +            num_retries=MAX_RETRIES,                          
      154 +            drop_params=True,                                 
      155 +            max_tokens=8192,  # headroom for ~N records (keep 
          +chunk_size <= ~15)                                            
      156 +            messages=[                                        
      157 +                {"role": "system", "content": SYSTEM_PROMPT + 
          +BATCH_SUFFIX},                                                
      158 +                {"role": "user", "content": user_message},    
      159 +            ],                                                
      160 +            response_format={                                 
      161 +                "type": "json_object",                        
      162 +                "response_schema": BatchPredictions.model_json
          +_schema(),                                                    
      163 +                "enforce_validation": True,                   
      164 +            },                                                
      165 +        )                                                     
      166 +    except HANDLED as e:                                      
      167 +        if getattr(e, "raw_response", None):                  
      168 +            logger.error("batch {} rejected raw response: {}",
          + ids, e.raw_response)                                         
      169 +        logger.error("{} for batch {} — gave up after retries"
          +, type(e).__name__, ids)                                      
      170 +        raise                                                 
      171 +    logger.info(                                              
      172 +        "batch telemetry: patients={} total_tokens={} cost_usd
          +={}",                                                         
      173 +        len(ids),                                             
      174 +        response.usage.total_tokens,                          
      175 +        getattr(response, "_hidden_params", {}).get("response_
          +cost"),                                                       
      176 +    )                                                         
      177 +    raw = response.choices[0].message.content                 
      178 +    logger.info("batch raw response: {}", raw)                
      179 +    labels = BatchPredictions.model_validate_json(raw).predict
          +ions                                                          
      180 +    for x in labels:                                          
      181 +        logger.info("extracted {} -> {}", x.patient_id, save(x
          +, out_dir))                                                   
      182 +    return labels

⏺ Gate fully green (5 passed, 4 skipped, ruff + ty clean). The chunked-Gemini
  feature is implemented and CLI-exposed.

  What's in (minimal, reusing the existing loguru logging — no new infra):
  - schema.py — BatchPredictions(predictions: list[PatientLabels]) (object-root
  wrapper).
  - extract.py — extract_batch(interviews, out_dir, model): one call for a
  chunk, BatchPredictions schema, max_tokens=8192, strict parse, per-patient
  save() + log. Telemetry line per chunk (batch telemetry: patients=N 
  total_tokens=… cost_usd=…). Plus the e.raw_response logging you wanted — both
  extract() and extract_batch() now log the model's rejected output on a schema
  failure.
  - main.py — run_chunked() + --chunk-size fire param.
  - tests/test_extract.py — offline BatchPredictions round-trip test (5 passed).

  You run it (1 chunk first, to test before the full 5):
  # 1 chunk of 10 → tests/, 1 Gemini request:
  uv run python src/main.py --chunk-size=10 --limit=10 --out-dir=data/out/tests
  # full 50 in 5 calls (well under 20/day) once the chunk looks right:
  uv run python src/main.py --chunk-size=10

  ▎ ★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────
  ▎ The whole feature is additive — extract() (the 1-patient path, your 
  ▎ test/eval gold standard) is untouched; extract_batch() is a sibling that 
  ▎ only changes how many transcripts per request. The two share save(), 
  ▎ SYSTEM_PROMPT, the HANDLED graceful path, and the loguru logging, so 
  ▎ monitoring is uniform: grep extract.log for batch telemetry: to sum tokens 
  ▎ and count calls/day. The one property the chunk trades away is per-record 
  ▎ isolation (strict parse → a malformed chunk fails all 10) — deliberately 
  ▎ minimal; the tolerant per-record parse is parked in TODO if a chunk actually
  ▎ fails on the real data.
  ▎ ─────────────────────────────────────────────────

  This is a clean commit point (chunking + the ollama graceful handling + the

⏺ Confirmed and verified — your incremental plan is now fully supported. Gate
  green (5 passed, 4 skipped), and the offset slicing maps exactly to your runs:

  offset=0  -> P001..P010   (run 1 — test this first)
  offset=10 -> P011..P020   (run 2)
  offset=40 -> P041..P050   (run 5)

  Your exact workflow:
  uv run python src/main.py --chunk-size=10 --limit=10              # run 1: 
  P001–P010 (1 call)
  # verify the 10 JSONs look right, THEN:
  uv run python src/main.py --chunk-size=10 --limit=10 --offset=10  # run 2: 
  P011–P020
  uv run python src/main.py --chunk-size=10 --limit=10 --offset=20  # run 3
  uv run python src/main.py --chunk-size=10 --limit=10 --offset=30  # run 4
  uv run python src/main.py --chunk-size=10 --limit=10 --offset=40  # run 5

  Each run is 1 Gemini request, persists its 10 JSONs immediately (save() per
  patient as the chunk parses), and the return line reports the span (e.g.
  extracted 10/10 (P011..P020) -> data/out) so you can track progress. 5 runs =
  5 requests, well under 20/day.

  ▎ ★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────
  ▎ Two distinct safety properties now compose. Persistence (extract_batch saves
  ▎ each patient the instant the chunk validates) means a completed chunk is 
  ▎ durable. --offset means you choose which slice to run, so you never re-spend
  ▎ a request on an already-saved chunk. Together they give you crash-safe, 
  ▎ resumable, pay-once extraction without any resume-state file — you are the 
  ▎ resume pointer (--offset). The one caveat to remember: a chunk is still 
  ▎ all-or-nothing (strict parse), so if run 3 fails validation you re-run just 
  ▎ run 3 (--offset=20), not the whole batch — exactly the blast-radius the 
  ▎ chunk size controls.
  ▎ ─────────────────────────────────────────────────

  Run 1 is the real test: it's the first time Gemini has to emit a valid
  10-record BatchPredictions (the thing the offline gate can't prove). Commit
  this first (chunking + --offset + ollama graceful handling + e.raw_response
  logging — all gate-green), then fire run 1 and check the 10 JSONs land in
  data/out/tests/ before committing to runs 2–5.

⏺ Gate green, CLI cleaned. Final surface is exactly the workflow — nothing
  extraneous:

  ┌──────────────┬──────────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────┐
  │     Flag     │           Default            │          Purpose           │
  ├──────────────┼──────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┤
  │ --model      │ gemini/gemini-2.5-flash-lite │ swap model (Gemini ↔       │
  │              │                              │ Ollama)                    │
  ├──────────────┼──────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┤
  │ --limit      │ None (all)                   │ slice size — run one chunk │
  │              │                              │  for testing/incremental   │
  ├──────────────┼──────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┤
  │              │                              │ which slice — incremental  │
  │ --offset     │ 0                            │ runs without re-doing      │
  │              │                              │ earlier chunks             │
  ├──────────────┼──────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┤
  │ --chunk-size │ 10                           │ transcripts per call       │
  ├──────────────┼──────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┤
  │ --out-dir    │ data/out                     │ route test runs to tests/  │
  └──────────────┴──────────────────────────────┴────────────────────────────┘

  Two modes, both clean:
  - Bare run-all: uv run python src/main.py → all 50 in chunks of 10 = 5 calls
  (the default).
  - Parameterised: --limit/--offset/--chunk-size/--model/--out-dir for
  incremental + testing.

  The default is now documented and justified in the main() docstring: chunks of
  10 → 5 calls, sized to the Gemini free tier's ~20 requests/day cap (taken as
  a fixed constraint; 50 one-at-a-time calls would blow it).

  Cleanup done: removed the dead run() function (per-call loop, used only by the
  old branch) and the now-unused extract import — main.py is purely the chunked
  coordinator.

  ▎ ★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────
  ▎ Defaulting chunk_size=10 flips the bare command from a footgun to the right 
  ▎ thing: previously uv run python src/main.py would have fired 50 individual 
  ▎ requests (instantly over the 20/day cap and failing ~#21); now it does the 
  ▎ quota-safe 5-call run. That's the value of encoding the constraint in the 
  ▎ default, not just a flag — the safe path is the one you get by doing nothing
  ▎ special, and you have to opt out (via --chunk-size=1) to do something 
  ▎ riskier. The docstring records why 10, so the next reader doesn't "optimise"
  ▎ it back to a footgun.
  ▎ ─────────────────────────────────────────────────

  Clean commit point: the chunking feature + --offset/--chunk-size CLI +
  default-run-all + ollama graceful handling + e.raw_response logging — all
  gate-green. After committing, run 1 (uv run python src/main.py --limit=10) is
  the real test: first time Gemini must emit a valid 10-record BatchPredictions.
  Check those 10 JSONs before runs 2–5.

  ▎ ★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────
  ▎ The one failure mode worth watching specifically across the runs is the 
  ▎ strict all-or-nothing chunk parse: if any single record in a batch of 10 
  ▎ drifts from the schema, the whole chunk raises JSONSchemaValidationError and
  ▎ none of those 10 persist — you'd see a graceful sys.exit with the 
  ▎ rate-limit/schema message and zero new files, not a partial write. So my 
  ▎ first check each time is simply "did 10 new files appear for the expected 
  ▎ span?" — if yes, the chunk validated cleanly end-to-end; if the run exited 
  ▎ with a schema message, we inspect e.raw_response in the log to see which 
  ▎ record drifted and re-run just that --offset.
